TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

The Houthis are causing major problems for Saudi Arabia. The Iran-backed militants made significant territorial gains along Yemen’s Red Sea coastline in September, and the Saudi-backed Yemeni government is struggling to push the Houthis back. Meanwhile, the group has launched deadly strikes in Saudi Arabia that have disrupted air travel and damaged key energy infrastructure. Riyadh is pushing for more international support as the conflict escalates. In a New York Times op-ed this week, the Saudi ambassador to the United States, Reema Bandar al-Saud, compared the Houthis to the Islamic State, saying that the Yemeni group is “underestimated” in the same way that the Islamic State was before it declared a caliphate. She urged the international community to “set a clear goal to end the Houthis as an armed terrorist force,” arguing that the Islamic State “was defeated by local forces backed by a coalition of more than 80 nations. The Houthis can be defeated the same way.” Defining the threat. Unlike the Islamic State, the Houthis do not seek to establish a global Islamic caliphate. Their objectives are less ambitious and are centered on conquering Yemen and removing Saudi influence from the country. But there’s no doubt that the Houthis pose a significant regional threat. Their asymmetrical tactics, including the use of low-cost drones, have proved to be a serious challenge even for a wealthy country with a sophisticated military like Saudi Arabia. And with Israel having severely degraded the capabilities of Hamas and Hezbollah over the last several years, the Houthis are now one of the most powerful Iranian proxies in the Middle East. * Is the UAE the Missing Link in the Fight Against the Houthis?
  Foreign Policy | 29 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Riyadh and Abu Dhabi reconciling could be a gamechanger, a leading Yemen expert says.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Saudi Arabia has a growing list of problems in its escalating war against the Houthis in Yemen, which has entered one of its deadliest chapters since it began more than a decade ago. The Iran-backed Houthis made major territorial gains along the Red Sea coast in a blistering offensive last month, and there’s been a sharp rise in deadly attacks on Saudi territory by the Houthis in recent weeks, including a strike on Oct. 10 on Riyadh’s King Khalid International Airport that killed at least 13 people and injured more than 300. Saudi Arabia has a growing list of problems in its escalating war against the Houthis in Yemen, which has entered one of its deadliest chapters since it began more than a decade ago. The Iran-backed Houthis made major territorial gains along the Red Sea coast in a blistering offensive last month, and there’s been a sharp rise in deadly attacks on Saudi territory by the Houthis in recent weeks, including a strike on Oct. 10 on Riyadh’s King Khalid International Airport that killed at least 13 people and injured more than 300. Saudi Arabia has struggled to convince international partners to join the fight or offer support beyond defensive assistance—with powerful allies such as the United States refusing to intervene. The Saudi-backed coalition fighting to push back the Houthis on the ground in Yemen has also had mixed success. A huge part of the problem in the fight is the lack of cohesion among anti-Houthi forces in southern Yemen, which is linked to tensions between Saudi Arabia and the United Arab Emirates. A leading Yemen expert said that Riyadh and Abu Dhabi reconciling—and leveraging both the UAE’s military effectiveness and influence over anti-Houthi Yemeni factions—could be a gamechanger in the fight. “It’s the most important thing that could happen to change the course of the battle on the ground,” said April Longley Alley, a senior fellow at The Washington Institute whose research focuses on Yemen and the Gulf. But it’s also a “complicated challenge to reconcile,” Alley said, due to “bitterness” between Riyadh and Abu Dhabi over what’s happened in Yemen, as well as tensions over Sudan and “wider regional competition.” The war in Yemen began in 2014, when the Houthis seized the capital, Sanaa, and overthrew the country’s government. In response, a Saudi-led coalition of Gulf countries, including the UAE, launched a military campaign against the group to try and restore the government.

* Experts: What China wants from the COP31 climate talks
  Carbon Brief | 29 Oct 14:25 UTC
  With the COP31 climate summit in Turkey looming, all eyes are on China to see what the country will push… The post Experts: What China wants from the COP31 climate talks appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: With the COP31 climate summit in Turkey looming, all eyes are on China to see what the country will push for in the negotiations. At COP30 last year, China helped to secure a “dialogue” on climate-related “ unilateral trade measures ”, as well as a two-year “work programme” concerning climate finance. The nation also championed south-south cooperation throughout the conference and co-led a summit on methane, alongside Brazil and the UK. In a recent press conference , China’s environment vice-minister Li Gao noted the need to “uphold multilateralism” and send a “clear political signal” that the global community is united around climate action. Li also pledged to advocate for an “open and efficient” trade system that “facilitates” adoption of clean-energy technologies. On the more traditional topics of mitigation and finance, Li highlighted the need to “address insufficient emission reduction efforts by developed countries and inadequate financial support” for developing countries. Carbon Brief asked six leading experts on China what they believe China wants from COP31 and what signals they will be watching for. Their responses have been edited for length and clarity. Director of the China climate hub and climate diplomacy, Asia Society Policy Institute In an era where global climate cooperation is severely challenged, China’s top priority at COP31 will be upholding multilateralism. With most “big ticket” items under the Paris Agreement now complete and constrained expectations for major new breakthroughs at COP31, China may be satisfied with a conference that produces no major drama and little else. Nevertheless, expect China’s vision for multilateralism to have a distinct flavor to it. Chinese officials may again leverage the COP stage to oppose unilateralism and promote an open and fair international trade system, reflecting Beijing’s persistent frustration with tariffs and other measures that have inhibited the global flow of Chinese clean technologies. One question is whether they will do this through existing spaces or push for a dedicated agenda item on trade, given that COP30’s trade outcomes, championed by China, mostly relegated discussions to mid-year technical meetings. China may also seek clarity on implementation pathways for existing targets, including nationally determined contributions (NDCs) and the $300bn climate finance goal . Amid US withdrawal , officials remain anxious about additional pressure that Beijing may receive. After vice-prem
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/experts-what-china-wants-from-the-cop31-climate-talks

* Analysis: China’s clean-tech exports in 2026 to avoid as much CO2 as Brazil emits in a year
  Carbon Brief | 29 Oct 07:51 UTC
  China’s exports in 2026 of clean-energy technologies, such as solar panels, batteries and electric vehicles, are on track to cut… The post Analysis: China’s clean-tech exports in 2026 to avoid as much CO2 as Brazil emits in a year appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: China’s exports in 2026 of clean-energy technologies, such as solar panels, batteries and electric vehicles, are on track to cut emissions beyond its borders by 2%, reveals new analysis. Lauri Myllyvirta, lead analyst at the Centre for Research on Energy and Clean Air and senior fellow at Asia Society Policy Institute The impact of these Chinese exports on carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions around the world grew by 26% year-on-year in the first three quarters of 2026, having already risen by 36% in 2025. The new analysis for Carbon Brief is based on detailed data for clean-tech product exports, the electricity system in destination countries and emissions during manufacturing in China. If the latest trends continue for the rest of the year, then Chinese clean-tech exports in 2026 alone will avoid around 450m tonnes of CO2 (MtCO2) – roughly equivalent to Brazil’s annual emissions. Other key findings from the analysis include: The analysis shows that most of China’s clean-energy exports have been built on new or expanded markets, enabling other producers to maintain their own sales volumes. In addition, clean-energy sales within China are larger than exports for most technologies, but a domestic solar slowdown could exacerbate trade tensions, if it continues. China has become the pre-eminent producer of clean-energy technologies, from solar panels and wind turbines through to batteries and electric vehicles. The majority of China’s clean-tech production is destined for the domestic market. But it has also become a major exporter – and these exports are avoiding significant amounts of CO2. Previous analysis for Carbon Brief showed that these clean-energy exports in 2024 would cut emissions in countries outside China by around 1%. The latest analysis shows that China’s clean-tech exports in 2026 are on track to avoid around 450MtCO2, as shown in the figure below, which is nearly 2% of global emissions outside China. It is also roughly equivalent to Brazil’s annual CO2 emissions from fossil fuels. The analysis accounts for the impact of the clean-tech exports in the destination country, as well as the emissions due to manufacturing the products in China. Looking at the full-year data for 2025, manufacturing clean-energy equipment resulted in emissions of around 170MtCO2 within China, while avoiding 370MtCO2 in other countries. This implies that, in aggregate, it takes six months for the exported goods to pay back the CO2 from manufacturing. China’s solar-equipment 
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/analysis-chinas-clean-tech-exports-in-2026-to-avoid-as-much-co2-as-brazil-emits-in-a-year

* Walmart Blocks Its Stock From OKX-ICE Tokenized Trading Venue
  Decrypt | 29 Oct 15:07 UTC [also carried by: CoinDesk]
  Walmart, Eli Lilly, Cigna, Carniva,l and Arcos Dorados objected to trading of their shares on the planned OKX-ICE tokenized stock venue, bringing the total to 38.
  FULL TEXT: The world’s largest retailer is now keeping its shares off OKX and ICE’s planned tokenized stock venue. Walmart, Eli Lilly, Cigna Group, Carnival, and Arcos Dorados have objected to having their shares offered on OKXICE TSV, according to an updated notice from the venue dated Wednesday. The five additions bring the total to 38 companies. That’s up from 33 a day earlier, when JPMorgan Chase, Coca-Cola and Dell were among seven newcomers. The tally has more than quadrupled since the venue’s first list of nine objectors on Oct. 15. It grew to 16 on Oct. 19, with names including Microsoft , and 22 on Oct. 23 when Wells Fargo joined the list . The latest batch broadens the pushback beyond tech and finance into retail, health care, and travel. Arcos Dorados, the largest McDonald’s franchisee in Latin America, joins McDonald’s itself, which objected earlier this month. Eli Lilly and Cigna join UnitedHealth Group and Moderna among the health care companies on the list. The objections carry real weight . Under the SEC’s innovation exemption , which the agency unveiled in September after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate, a venue must notify a company before offering a version of its shares tokenized by an unaffiliated third party. If the company objects in writing within 30 days, the venue can’t make that stock available for trading, according to the SEC’s order . The objection applies only to that venue, and the notices don’t disclose the companies’ reasons. OKXICE TSV, a 50-50 joint venture between NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange and OKX’s U.S. holding company, plans to let approved users trade tokenized U.S. stocks around the clock. OKX and ICE first teamed up on tokenized securities earlier this year before announcing the 24/7 venue this month. Trades would run through permissioned Uniswap v4 pools on OKX’s XLayer blockchain, paired with stablecoins such as USDC. The exemption caps the venue at 75 of the largest, most liquid stocks, with trading limited to 0.25% of each stock’s average daily volume, and up to 250 other stocks at 2.5%. The list may not be finished. Under the SEC’s order, companies have 30 days from receiving notice to object, and some of the market’s most heavily traded names, including Nvidia, Tesla, Apple and Amazon, haven’t done so. The venue has also said it can list shares tokenized by the issuers themselves, which would give companies direct control over how their stock appears onchain, though the notices don’t identify any such l
  https://decrypt.co/382145/walmart-blocks-stock-okx-ice-tokenized-trading-venue

  FULL TEXT: On the far eastern edge of Cairo, on Oct. 27, 2026, the New Cairo Misdemeanour Court (NCMC) was the scene of two events that will shape the future of independent journalism in Egypt. In a fourth-floor courtroom, the judge asked the litigants to leave their seats and clear the room for three minutes. Three minutes, it turned out, were all he needed to remove one of Egypt’s most prominent independent news outlets from the scene. He fined Lina Attalah, the editor-in-chief of Mada Masr, 3 million Egyptian pounds (around $53,450) for running a website without a license, and ordered the closure of the outlet that had fended off this fate for more than 13 years, holding firm against website blocking and official harassment of its journalists. At the same time, three floors away in the same building, lawyers were gathering in another courtroom for a detention-renewal hearing for six journalists: the entire newsroom of Matsda2sh (“Don’t Believe It”), a platform that specializes in fact-checking and verifying news. Over videoconference, the Supreme State Security Prosecution (SSSP) extended their detention by 15 days on top of the time they had been held since Sept. 28, 2026, as they faced charges including joining a terrorist group and spreading false news. Together, the two cases illustrate how authorities are targeting independent media through different legal mechanisms, from licensing prosecutions to national security charges. The scenes sum up the state of independent journalism in Egypt, amid a fraught political climate and mixed signals that swing between a security clampdown and the prospect of a loosening of restrictions. A clear pattern stands out: official rhetoric warning of threats to national security has risen in lockstep with an escalating campaign against independent media. Since Oct. 14, 2026, President Abdel Fattah el-Sisi has issued a string of warnings about what he called attempts to “target national consciousness and tamper with Egyptians’ collective memory” and to “bring down states from within,” as well as “disinformation campaigns targeting Egyptians’ will.” These warnings came alongside his announcement that his presidency will end in 2030, and his remarks about a detailed plan, along with a program run by the Military Academy, to train future leaders and prepare potential successors. In the nearly two years since the Bashar al-Assad regime fell, the interim authorities have signed two large real estate deals with Saudi developers on land in the western suburbs of Damascus that the old regime took from farmers in the 1970s and 1980s: Abyat Hills in Qudsaya and Sham View between Qudsaya and Dummar. In late September, the original owners marched on both sites and stopped the machinery. The government responded that the expropriations were legal and all requisite compensation had been paid. Even for people from the area like me, the protests and the public debate since the downfall of the Assad regime have revealed the scale of the issue in ways we had not previously understood. In the towns along the Damascus-Beirut highway alone, residents count tens of thousands of acres taken for state housing under two decrees signed by Hafez al-Assad in the mid-1970s, with thousands more in Dummar. The Assad regime used the expropriation law to build military bases, security branches and loyalist housing around the Syrian capital. Much of the land the regime took is still in the state’s hands, either fenced off or built on. The new authorities have inherited that issue, but, to the farmers of my father’s generation, they have so far shown no intention of correcting a basic injustice. My own family, my father and his three brothers, lost about 50 acres spread across all three towns. They had bought the land with their hard-earned money while their widowed mother was alive. The distinction that will ultimately matter is … whether AI can serve its purpose and be controlled locally. Currently, the conversation on AI agents revolves around China versus the U.S. , open versus closed models, and who will dominate the AI race. But in Africa’s capitals, the distinction that will ultimately matter is not whose product was used to build a system, or who dominates whom, but rather whether AI can serve its purpose and be controlled locally. So how can Africa build with agency, and with control over the code and makeup of its own systems, even though they are built on foreign technology? African countries are already building AI for digital transformation, as outlined in the African Union’s continental AI strategy and country-specific AI strategies. Policymakers are building AI infrastructure but rarely discuss how AI delivers economic growth in a safe environment. Developers across Africa are creating AI-powered innovations that sit on the continent’s digital infrastructure — chief among them is mobile money. These innovations bring new opportunities, but also new threats. In July, AI agents running inside an OpenAI evaluation attacked the production infrastructure of Hugging Face. In September, rogue OpenAI agents breached Australian government websites. Africa, the home of mobile money, which has brought financial inclusion to hundreds of millions, may be the next target. In countries like Kenya and Ghana, mobile money is not simply another digital service; it is infrastructure for everyday economic activity.   FULL TEXT: The senior partner didn’t waste any time. ‘I’m sorry, Zahaan,’ he said, ‘your services are no longer required at the firm.’ We sometimes called him the Burger King behind his back, because he ate a Whopper Meal at his desk every day. I’d never spoken to him before, and now I never would again. It was the summer of 2001. I was fresh out of university and nine months into my first job as a management consultant in London. My parents had allowed me to move back into my old room, where I routinely collapsed after getting home from the office well after midnight. Professional life was punishing, but I gave it my all. But then I got a phone call, telling me that one of the senior American partners wanted to meet me. That was unusual; but then again, I’d been working so hard and getting such good feedback, I figured it couldn’t be anything too bad. I was directed to an office on the first floor, several levels below where I usually sat. After he fired me, my ears buzzed and my chest tightened; I don’t remember much else, except being told that I shouldn’t even return to my desk. It became apparent that the location of the meeting had been chosen because it would allow me to take a single flight of stairs down to the ground floor. No one would need to see me, and I wouldn’t need to speak to anyone. The decision felt cruel and unfair. But more than anything, it seemed to violate the laws that for many years had been my way of making sense of the world: the laws of physics. Cause and effect, action and reaction. What followed was an unlikely journey – one in which the equations I thought I’d left behind at university became, unexpectedly, a companion through the messiest parts of life. While I’ll be forever grateful for the opportunities my education afforded me, life still felt tough for me as a lonely and anxious teenager. The school’s social currency was confidence, quick comebacks, and an effortless cool I never quite mastered. I often felt like I was watching from the edges, trying to decode the rules of a game that everyone else grasped intuitively. The only thing that got me through was physics. * The Amarna letters
  Aeon | 28 Oct 10:01 UTC
  Petty disputes, pleas for help and requests for gold – explore letters sent to Egyptian pharaohs more than 3,000 years ago - by Aeon Video Watch on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: First discovered in Egypt in the late 19th century, the collection of clay tablets known as the Amarna letters is one of our richest windows into the ancient world. The letters are extraordinary not only for their contents – diplomatic communications exchanged between Egyptian pharaohs and other nearby rulers in the 14th century BCE – but also for their script: they were written not in Egyptian hieroglyphs but largely in cuneiform, a writing system most often associated with ancient Mesopotamia. In this video from the British Museum, the always entertaining Assyriologist Irving Finkel details how the letters came to be and how they were discovered, and delves into the fascinating correspondence itself, which includes petty disputes over perceived slights, leaders lobbying for military support and more than a few requests for gold. The post Serbia’s Victorious New Democrats Face an Old Question appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: For the second time in just over a quarter-century, Serbia has seemingly thrown off the yoke of one-man rule. In October 2000, Serbians mobilized — first at the ballot box, then in the streets — to oust the genocidal regime of Slobodan Milošević . In October 2026, it was sufficient for them merely to vote in overwhelming numbers to force the concession of the strongman at the helm. This time Serbians ousted Milošević’s protege, Aleksandar Vučić, and his ruling Serbian Progressive Party (SNS) after their nearly 14-year tenure in government. The vote capped two years of protest that began in November 2024, when a newly renovated canopy at Novi Sad’s railway station collapsed, killing 16 people. An insurgent student movement blamed the deaths on government corruption, which they saw as a hallmark of Vučić’s rule, and their ensuing blockades of universities grew into the largest protest movement in Serbia since 2000. The movement eventually coalesced into the so-called Student List parliamentary coalition. On Sunday, this bloc won 53% of the popular vote and secured around 140 seats in the country’s 250-seat National Assembly, providing it with a comfortable, stable governing majority. Domestically, the Student List ran on a sweeping anti-corruption platform. Its foreign policy agenda, however, is virtually indistinguishable from that of the SNS — at least on paper. Why? Because Serbian nationalism remains, arguably, the most potent political force in the country. The vote that propelled the Serbian List into power is a heterodox amalgam of the country’s moderate, liberal public and the antiregime far right (voters who believe Vučić has been insufficiently hard-line, for instance, in opposing Kosovo’s sovereignty, and would like the country to pursue still deeper ties with Moscow). The same curious combination was evident throughout the two-year protest movement preceding the vote. A very similar coalition toppled Milošević in 2000. This is the bind in which Serbia’s would-be democrats find themselves. Their aspirations to remake the country’s political order depend, in part, on the support of some of the region’s most reactionary elements. Yet the past 40 years suggest that, much as in Russia, Serbia’s regional ambitions and democratization at home are at odds. The reason is institutional. * Love with nowhere to go
  Aeon | 27 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Grief is not only for the dead. When we are unable to honour other profound losses, we become strangers to ourselves - by Derek van Zoonen Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Telemark, Norway, 2011. Photo by Jonas Bendiksen/Magnum Photos I have been grieving my mother for years, even though she is still alive. Sometimes before falling asleep – in that liminal space between waking and sleeping where the self becomes porous – I’d see the image of the sweet and caring woman who would come to say goodnight, her cheeks covered in thick layers of night cream. Then, out of nowhere, the image would flip into the raging woman who was so entangled in her own suffering that she turned her frustration on us. Over the years, that second woman slowly swallowed the first, as she got pulled into my brother’s addiction. It’s hard to say where her own unhappiness ended and my brother’s suffering began, but together they drifted into a parallel reality, cut off from the world beyond their heads. There was no funeral for this. No condolence cards. No flowers. No phone calls. No leave of absence. What was I supposed to mourn? And yet what I felt for them, what I still feel, has the unmistakable gravity of mourning. Can there be grief without death? I have come to think that we grieve much more often than we realise, and that death is grief’s most visible occasion rather than its essence. If that doesn’t match your experience, it may be because we rarely call these other losses by their name. Most of our losses arrive without a funeral. That is the human predicament: we are creatures built to attach, living in a world where nothing stays. Sooner or later, we lose the things we care about. And since we are complex beings with a body, a mind, a community and an environment – beings who love many different things – there is a great deal we can lose, from our physical health to our sense of who we are, from relationships to homelands to the natural world we share with other species. For creatures like us, there is no way around heartbreak: to live is to lose. Grief is what love does when its object is gone. It is love with nowhere to go. Over the past few years I have accumulated many such losses. There is the psychological loss of my father as I knew and loved him: a brutal stroke rendered him incapable of walking and cycling, and cycling was the only thing that could ‘make his thoughts liquid’, as he used to say. There is the loss of old friendships. First you are no longer part of each other’s lives – you just update one another about your life – and then, one day, it becomes awkward to reach out at all.   https://aeon.co/essays/we-need-a-language-to-name-the-grief-that-comes-without-death

My first book, Dreamers, closed with a chapter on fake call centers in India; my second, Scamlands, traveled to other low-income countries in Asia to portray an invisible empire that thrives on inequality, technological change and the erosion of trust. Along the way, I met and interviewed the scammers whose labor keeps this empire running. For some, fraud was a route to upward mobility; for others, an act of revenge against the privileged. Though I have formally emerged from this underworld, I can never delete a scam email without wanting to know more. “An ambitious young couple, a multimillion-dollar swindle and the sting that brought them down.” “For years, North Korea has been secretly placing young IT workers inside Western companies. With AI, their schemes are now more devious—and effective—than ever.” “How the RCMP busted the biggest fraud ever to target Canadians—and why they can’t keep up anymore.”

Until the fall of the Assad regime, those conditions were generally respected, though there were occasional violations. Saddam is one of 40 Syrians who remain in Israel after a forcible transfer across state borders, considered a war crime under the Geneva Convention.   FULL TEXT: In early September, images of huge queues forming outside Iraqi gas stations spread across the country like wildfire. * AI models cost more when you don’t use English
  Rest of World | 26 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Some languages require far more tokens for AI models to process, raising costs and reducing context for the people who use them.
  FULL TEXT: People who use less widely spoken languages could end up paying more to use artificial intelligence than those who speak English or other dominant languages. A new study published last month by the Coalition for Language Equity in AI Research found AI models require far more tokens for some languages than others. Tokens are the units of text or data that the models process. The researchers have called this a hidden “tokenizer tax,” which can make AI more expensive in languages with smaller online presence. A tokenizer is a tool that breaks down raw text, images, or audio into tokens. Even within the 20 most popular languages on the internet, there is a gap in token usage, the research found. The most expensive of the languages used between 1.5 and 5 times more tokens than the cheapest. This gap widened when researchers expanded the set to 200 languages, with the token cost gap in one AI model growing by 17 times. “A startup in [Bengaluru] may pay a surcharge on every prompt and completion while its competitor in Austin does not,” the study said. “The tokenizer tax also has implications beyond cost. Higher token counts translate into higher latency and reduced effective context capacity. These penalties hit hardest where compute and connectivity are already limited. Tokenizer inequity is a constraint on who can participate in the AI economy.” Non-English languages leave less room for documents or conversation history, and require more work to generate the answer, according to Pratyush Choudhury, co-founder of Activate AI, India’s first venture capital fund dedicated exclusively to AI. Activate backs multilingual voice AI startups like ElevenLabs, Sarvam, and Wispr Flow. “Think about a tutor: Retaining the student’s earlier mistakes and explanations is part of delivering a good lesson. If that history consumes the context window much faster in one language, the disadvantage reaches into the quality of the service,” he said. One way to improve language processing is to improve the underlying data. “Open data efforts matter here, like RESPIN, a collaborative initiative with [the Indian Institute of Science] funded by the Gates Foundation, where we collected text from native speakers of 38 dialects along with read speech,” Raoul Nanavati, co-founder and CEO of Mumbai-based AI voice infrastructure startup Navana.ai , told Rest of World . However, the study by the Coalition for Language Equity in AI Research found the rarity of a language isn’t the only determina
  https://restofworld.org/2026/ai-tokenizer-tax-non-english-languages-cost-inequity/?utm_source=rss&utm_medium=rss&utm_campaign=feeds

I moved to Latin America, to complete my doctoral studies and study revolutionary movements in that part of the world, grappling all the while with a question that had become inseparable from my own existence: how could peace be built in Kurdistan? * A Change at a Damascus School Has Syrians Reading the Runes
  New Lines | 23 Oct 10:24 UTC
  A hundred years after its founding, the elite, secular Laique school in Damascus has decided to separate boys and girls for the first time. Syrians are asking what that means for the school, and the future of Syria. The post A Change at a Damascus School Has Syrians Reading the Runes appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: Exactly 100 years ago, a secular French school opened in Damascus with 79 students, in rented premises close to where the current parliament stands on Abid Street. The school was known then as the Laique Institute and is known today as the Al-Hurriya Institute, but is generally referred to as just Laique. Four years later, in 1930, then-Prime Minister Taj al-Din al-Hasani, a turbaned cleric with a reformist agenda, granted the school a plot of land on Baghdad Street, to which it formally relocated in 1932. Laique quickly became one of the most prestigious institutions in Syria, celebrated for its coeducation, the quality of its faculty, and its tennis, volleyball and basketball courts. It also had an impressive library, along with a full-fledged theater that once hosted Egyptian diva Umm Kulthum in 1955. That colorful tapestry was part of a Middle East that imagined itself as part of a broader Arab world with a united identity. Fast forward 100 years, and Laique has become a shadow of its former self. First came 61 years of Baath Party rule that imposed an indoctrinating socialist curriculum on the entire Syrian school system, along with khaki uniforms. Teachers at Laique were hired not because they were good, but because they were Baathists. Then came the 1967 nationalization that ended the school’s autonomy and distorted its institutional identity, renaming it “Mahad al-Huriyya,” or the “Institute of Freedom,” a nod to the language of Baathism, with its three ideological pillars of freedom, pan-Arabism and socialism. The fact that Hafez al-Assad chose Laique for all his children — Bashar included — only hardened the school’s Baathism. When Assad’s eldest son and chosen successor, Bassel al-Assad, died in 1994, the school was renamed after him. What did not disappear was the memory of what Laique had been, and, more importantly, its alumni network of engineers, doctors, politicians and artists who became the “who’s who” of Syrian society. For decades, “I went to Laique” functioned as a kind of passport, akin to “I went to Brummana High School” in Lebanon: a signal of academic excellence that guaranteed entry into the workforce and came with high expectations of talent and character. A great deal of Syrian history was compressed into a single address. This was a school founded under the French mandate, legitimized by a reformist cleric premier, staffed by French and Syrian teachers, and attended by the children of the urban bourgeoisie.
